Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, my primary doctor suggested I come in. I’m here because... well, for over a year now, I’ve been trying to get someone, anyone really, to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work. I've been in computer support for a long time, you know, over thirty years. Back in the nineties, we didn't have these automated ticketing systems. We actually walked over to people's desks to fix things. I remember I had this one user, Susan, who used to bring in homemade zucchini bread every Tuesday. She used walnuts, which I thought was a nice touch. You just don't see that kind of office culture anymore. Now everyone just stares at their screens and drinks that terrible pod coffee in the breakroom. I actually brought in my own French press last week, but someone complained to HR about the grounds in the sink. People are so petty. But anyway, management keeps pulling me in, showing me these system logs and saying the new ticket assignments are just company policy, but I know what they're really doing. I mean, if you're going to complain about coffee grounds, at least have the courage to leave a note, right?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, absolutely I believe it. Like I was just saying, it's been going on for over a year now. They pull me into these little conference rooms and point at all these system logs, giving me this rehearsed speech about how the new ticket rotation and the security audits are just standard company policy. But I know a paper trail when I see one. 

Speaking of paper, we used to use these massive dot-matrix printers back at my first job out of college. You remember those? The noise they made was incredible, just this loud mechanical screeching, and you had to carefully tear off those perforated edges with the little holes in them. I actually kept a box of that old continuous-feed paper in my garage for the longest time. My ex-wife used to get so annoyed that I was taking up space with it, but it was perfect for making banners for the kids' birthdays. I made my daughter this huge banner for her tenth birthday, must have been fifteen feet long. She was completely obsessed with horses that year. We even took her to this farm out in the county where she got to ride a pony named Macaroni... 

Wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the logs and the audits. Yeah, management can call it policy all they want, but I'm not stupid. I know exactly what they're building against me.

No, I don't think so. I mean, it's just how conversations work, isn't it? One thing reminds you of another. Like, you mention printers, and it just takes me right back. Honestly, I think people today are too focused on getting straight to the point. They don't appreciate a good story. 

My grandfather, now he was a storyteller. He used to sit on the porch for hours, whittling these little wooden figures—mostly bears and eagles—and he'd tell us these grand tales about his time in the Navy. He was stationed in the Pacific, you know. Always said the humidity out there would rust a man's bones if he wasn't careful. He brought back this beautiful carved wooden box from the Philippines. My sister has it now. She always was his favorite, which I never really understood because she never even liked the wooden figures he made. She was more into collecting those porcelain dolls. You know the ones with the creepy glass eyes that seem to follow you around the room? My grandmother had a whole cabinet full of them, and I swear, I couldn't sleep when we visited because I thought they were watching me. 

But anyway, no, I don't think I jump around. I'm just trying to give you the full picture of who I am. You can't understand a person if you only look at one little piece of them, right? Just like you can't understand what management is doing if you only look at one ticket. You have to see the whole pattern.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I don't know if anyone else sees it. I mean, nobody's come out and said it to me. But honestly, most of them are so young they wouldn't know a targeted audit if it bit them. They're all fresh out of those boot camps, you know? Six weeks of coding and suddenly they're system engineers. 

When I went to college, we actually had to learn the theory behind it all. I remember my first computer science professor, Dr. Henderson. He had this massive, unruly beard and always wore these tweed jackets with elbow patches. Classic academic type. He used to say that if you didn't understand how the hardware worked, you had no business writing software. He actually made us build a simple logic board from scratch before we ever touched a keyboard. I burned my fingers on the soldering iron three times that semester. Still have a tiny scar on my left thumb from it. 

I actually tried to teach my son how to solder when he was in middle school, but he just wasn't interested. He was all about his skateboard. He broke his arm trying to do some trick off a set of stairs at the park. We spent six hours in the emergency room that night. The vending machine was completely out of everything except those weird cheese crackers that taste like cardboard. 

Anyway, what was I... oh, right, the other people at work. Yeah, I don't think they see the pattern. They just blindly follow the ticket system and don't question why I'm getting all the high-risk assignments. They're too busy talking about whatever new app they downloaded.

Honestly, I think people today are too focused on getting straight to the point. They don't appreciate a good story. \n\nMy grandfather, now he was a storyteller. He used to sit on the porch for hours, whittling these little wooden figures—mostly bears and eagles—and he'd tell us these grand tales about his time in the Navy. He was stationed in the Pacific, you know. Always said the humidity out there would rust a man's bones if he wasn't careful. He brought back this beautiful carved wooden box from the Philippines. You know the ones with the creepy glass eyes that seem to follow you around the room? Six weeks of coding and suddenly they're system engineers. \n\nWhen I went to college, we actually had to learn the theory behind it all. I remember my first computer science professor, Dr. Henderson. He had this massive, unruly beard and always wore these tweed jackets with elbow patches. Classic academic type. He used to say that if you didn't understand how the hardware worked, you had no business writing software. He actually made us build a simple logic board from scratch before we ever touched a keyboard. I burned my fingers on the soldering iron three times that semester. Still have a tiny scar on my left thumb from it. \n\nI actually tried to teach my son how to solder when he was in middle school, but he just wasn't interested. He was all about his skateboard. The vending machine was completely out of everything except those weird cheese crackers that taste like cardboard. \n\nAnyway, what was I... oh, right, the other people at work. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I don't know why me, specifically. I mean, I'm good at my job, I've been there forever. Maybe I know too much about the legacy systems. They hate the legacy systems. They want everything in the cloud now. 

I remember when "the cloud" just meant it was going to rain. My grandmother used to say she could smell the rain coming, and she was always right. She grew up on a farm in Nebraska, out where the sky is just... huge. You can see weather rolling in from miles away. We drove out there once when I was a kid, took the station wagon all the way across the country. That car had those wood panels on the sides, you know the ones? And no air conditioning. My brother and I stuck to the vinyl seats the whole way through Kansas. We played the license plate game for hours, trying to find all fifty states. I think we only ever missed Hawaii and Alaska. We did see a plate from Guam once, though, in a diner parking lot in Omaha. The diner had the best cherry pie I've ever eaten. Real cherries, not that canned syrup stuff. 

But, uh... wait. Targeting me. Right. I don't know the exact reason. I just know they're doing it. You don't build a paper trail that thick unless you're trying to push someone out.

He broke his arm trying to do some trick off a set of stairs at the park. We spent six hours in the emergency room that night. The vending machine was completely out of everything except those weird cheese crackers that taste like cardboard. \n\nAnyway, what was I... oh, right, the other people at work. Yeah, I don't think they see the pattern. They just blindly follow the ticket system and don't question why I'm getting all the high-risk assignments. They're too busy talking about whatever new app they downloaded.\n\nDoctor: Do you think management is specifically targeting you for any particular reason?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know why me, specifically. I mean, I'm good at my job, I've been there forever. Maybe I know too much about the legacy systems. They hate the legacy systems. They want everything in the cloud now. \n\nI remember when \"the cloud\" just meant it was going to rain. My grandmother used to say she could smell the rain coming, and she was always right. She grew up on a farm in Nebraska, out where the sky is just... huge. You can see weather rolling in from miles away. We drove out there once when I was a kid, took the station wagon all the way across the country. That car had those wood panels on the sides, you know the ones? And no air conditioning. My brother and I stuck to the vinyl seats the whole way through Kansas. We played the license plate game for hours, trying to find all fifty states. I think we only ever missed Hawaii and Alaska. We did see a plate from Guam once, though, in a diner parking lot in Omaha. The diner had the best cherry pie I've ever eaten. Real cherries, not that canned syrup stuff. \n\nBut, uh... wait. Targeting me. Right. I don't know the exact reason. I just know they're doing it. You don't build a paper trail that thick unless you're trying to push someone out.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think management's actions are intended to harm you personally?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, harm me personally? I don't know if they want to physically hurt me, but they definitely want to ruin my career. I'm here because for over a year now, I've been trying to get someone to take this seriously. The new ticket rotation, those random security audits... they're using them to build this massive paper trail against me. 

And they keep pulling me into these meetings, showing me all these system logs and telling me, "Oh, it's just standard policy, everyone goes through this." But I know it's not true. 

Speaking of policies, my homeowners association just passed this ridiculous new rule about the height of the grass. They sent out this newsletter with a literal ruler printed on the side of it, like we're supposed to go out there and measure our lawns every Tuesday. The guy who runs the HOA, Bob, he's got way too much time on his hands since he retired from the post office. He used to be a mail carrier in my old neighborhood, actually. Walked the same route for thirty years. He always had these little dog treats in his pocket. My old golden retriever, Buster, used to wait by the window every afternoon just for Bob. Buster was such a good dog. We got him from a shelter when he was already three years old. He had this funny little limp on his back left leg from an old injury, but it didn't slow him down one bit. He loved swimming in the lake behind our house. 

But, yeah, the management at work. They say it's just policy, but I know what they're doing. They're trying to push me out, and I just want someone to finally listen to me about it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A larger conspiracy? I mean... I haven't seen anything to suggest that it goes beyond my department. I don't think the government or the Illuminati or whoever cares about a legacy systems guy at a mid-level tech firm. It's just my management. 

Although, speaking of conspiracies, my brother-in-law, Dave, he's completely obsessed with all that UFO stuff. He spends hours on these internet forums reading about declassified military videos. Last Thanksgiving, he brought this huge binder full of printed out articles and tried to show them to everyone while we were eating the turkey. My sister was so embarrassed, she kept trying to change the subject to her new garden. She planted these heirloom tomatoes last spring, the big purple ones? Cherokee Purples, I think they're called. They make the best sandwiches. Just some thick white bread, a little mayonnaise, some salt and pepper. You don't need anything else. I used to grow tomatoes when I had my first house, but the squirrels kept getting to them before they were ripe. Little thieves. I tried putting up netting, I tried that pepper spray stuff you put on the leaves, nothing worked. They just chewed right through the nets. 

But no, I don't think there's any grand conspiracy. It's just the people above me at work trying to force me out so they can hire someone cheaper who doesn't know any better.

My ex-wife used to get so annoyed that I was taking up space with it, but it was perfect for making banners for the kids' birthdays. I made my daughter this huge banner for her tenth birthday, must have been fifteen feet long. She was completely obsessed with horses that year. We even took her to this farm out in the county where she got to ride a pony named Macaroni... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the logs and the audits. Yeah, management can call it policy all they want, but I'm not stupid. I know exactly what they're building against me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself jumping from one topic to another like you did just now, from talking about work to talking about printers and your daughter's birthday?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, it's just how conversations work, isn't it? One thing reminds you of another. Like, you mention printers, and it just takes me right back. Honestly, I think people today are too focused on getting straight to the point. They don't appreciate a good story. \n\nMy grandfather, now he was a storyteller. He used to sit on the porch for hours, whittling these little wooden figures—mostly bears and eagles—and he'd tell us these grand tales about his time in the Navy. He was stationed in the Pacific, you know. Always said the humidity out there would rust a man's bones if he wasn't careful. He brought back this beautiful carved wooden box from the Philippines. My sister has it now. She always was his favorite, which I never really understood because she never even liked the wooden figures he made. She was more into collecting those porcelain dolls. You know the ones with the creepy glass eyes that seem to follow you around the room? My grandmother had a whole cabinet full of them, and I swear, I couldn't sleep when we visited because I thought they were watching me. \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't think I jump around. I'm just trying to give you the full picture of who I am. You can't understand a person if you only look at one little piece of them, right? Just like you can't understand what management is doing if you only look at one ticket. You have to see the whole pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you think others at your workplace share your belief that management is building a paper trail against you?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know if anyone else sees it. I mean, nobody's come out and said it to me. But honestly, most of them are so young they wouldn't know a targeted audit if it bit them. They're all fresh out of those boot camps, you know? Six weeks of coding and suddenly they're system engineers. \n\nWhen I went to college, we actually had to learn the theory behind it all. I remember my first computer science professor, Dr. Henderson. He had this massive, unruly beard and always wore these tweed jackets with elbow patches. Classic academic type. He used to say that if you didn't understand how the hardware worked, you had no business writing software. He actually made us build a simple logic board from scratch before we ever touched a keyboard. I burned my fingers on the soldering iron three times that semester. Still have a tiny scar on my left thumb from it. \n\nI actually tried to teach my son how to solder when he was in middle school, but he just wasn't interested. He was all about his skateboard. He broke his arm trying to do some trick off a set of stairs at the park. We spent six hours in the emergency room that night. The vending machine was completely out of everything except those weird cheese crackers that taste like cardboard. \n\nAnyway, what was I... oh, right, the other people at work. Yeah, I don't think they see the pattern. They just blindly follow the ticket system and don't question why I'm getting all the high-risk assignments. They're too busy talking about whatever new app they downloaded.\n\nDoctor: Do you think management is specifically targeting you for any particular reason?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know why me, specifically. I mean, I'm good at my job, I've been there forever. Maybe I know too much about the legacy systems. They hate the legacy systems. They want everything in the cloud now. \n\nI remember when \"the cloud\" just meant it was going to rain. My grandmother used to say she could smell the rain coming, and she was always right. She grew up on a farm in Nebraska, out where the sky is just... huge. You can see weather rolling in from miles away. We drove out there once when I was a kid, took the station wagon all the way across the country. That car had those wood panels on the sides, you know the ones? And no air conditioning. My brother and I stuck to the vinyl seats the whole way through Kansas. We played the license plate game for hours, trying to find all fifty states. I think we only ever missed Hawaii and Alaska. We did see a plate from Guam once, though, in a diner parking lot in Omaha. The diner had the best cherry pie I've ever eaten. Real cherries, not that canned syrup stuff. \n\nBut, uh... wait. Targeting me. Right. I don't know the exact reason. I just know they're doing it. You don't build a paper trail that thick unless you're trying to push someone out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think management's actions are intended to harm you personally?\n\nPatient: Well, harm me personally? I don't know if they want to physically hurt me, but they definitely want to ruin my career. I'm here because for over a year now, I've been trying to get someone to take this seriously. The new ticket rotation, those random security audits... they're using them to build this massive paper trail against me. \n\nAnd they keep pulling me into these meetings, showing me all these system logs and telling me, \"Oh, it's just standard policy, everyone goes through this.\" But I know it's not true. \n\nSpeaking of policies, my homeowners association just passed this ridiculous new rule about the height of the grass. They sent out this newsletter with a literal ruler printed on the side of it, like we're supposed to go out there and measure our lawns every Tuesday. The guy who runs the HOA, Bob, he's got way too much time on his hands since he retired from the post office. He used to be a mail carrier in my old neighborhood, actually. Walked the same route for thirty years. He always had these little dog treats in his pocket. My old golden retriever, Buster, used to wait by the window every afternoon just for Bob. Buster was such a good dog. We got him from a shelter when he was already three years old. He had this funny little limp on his back left leg from an old injury, but it didn't slow him down one bit. He loved swimming in the lake behind our house. \n\nBut, yeah, the management at work. They say it's just policy, but I know what they're doing. They're trying to push me out, and I just want someone to finally listen to me about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think management's actions against you are part of a larger conspiracy?\n\nPatient: A larger conspiracy? I mean... I haven't seen anything to suggest that it goes beyond my department. I don't think the government or the Illuminati or whoever cares about a legacy systems guy at a mid-level tech firm. It's just my management. \n\nAlthough, speaking of conspiracies, my brother-in-law, Dave, he's completely obsessed with all that UFO stuff. He spends hours on these internet forums reading about declassified military videos. Last Thanksgiving, he brought this huge binder full of printed out articles and tried to show them to everyone while we were eating the turkey. My sister was so embarrassed, she kept trying to change the subject to her new garden. She planted these heirloom tomatoes last spring, the big purple ones? Cherokee Purples, I think they're called. They make the best sandwiches. Just some thick white bread, a little mayonnaise, some salt and pepper. You don't need anything else. I used to grow tomatoes when I had my first house, but the squirrels kept getting to them before they were ripe. Little thieves. I tried putting up netting, I tried that pepper spray stuff you put on the leaves, nothing worked. They just chewed right through the nets. \n\nBut no, I don't think there's any grand conspiracy. It's just the people above me at work trying to force me out so they can hire someone cheaper who doesn't know any better.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Prove it to me? I mean... how could they? I've been doing this for a year. A whole year of trying to get someone, anyone, to take this seriously. They already show me the system logs, they already sit there in those little glass conference rooms and tell me, "Oh, it's just policy, everyone has the same ticket rotation, everyone gets these security audits." But I know what I'm looking at. They're just using it to build that paper trail against me. 

You know, those glass conference rooms are terrible for acoustics. You can hear everything from the hallway. We had this one manager, Greg, who used to chew ice during meetings. Just crunch, crunch, crunch, right into the microphone on conference calls. It drove everyone crazy. Greg ended up leaving to start a microbrewery up in Vermont or somewhere like that. He was always talking about hops. I don't really get the appeal of those super bitter IPAs, honestly. I prefer a nice, simple lager if I'm going to have a beer. My dad used to drink Pabst Blue Ribbon. He'd sit on the porch after work with a can of PBR and listen to the baseball game on this tiny little transistor radio he had. The reception was awful, just static half the time, but he knew all the players' stats by heart. He could tell you who was batting just by the sound of the announcer's voice through the fuzz. 

But... no, to answer your question, I wouldn't accept it. Because they already try to prove it to me with their logs and their policies, and I know they're lying. The paper trail is real, and it's aimed right at me.

I mean, it's just how conversations work, isn't it? One thing reminds you of another. Like, you mention printers, and it just takes me right back. Honestly, I think people today are too focused on getting straight to the point. They don't appreciate a good story. \n\nMy grandfather, now he was a storyteller. He used to sit on the porch for hours, whittling these little wooden figures—mostly bears and eagles—and he'd tell us these grand tales about his time in the Navy. He was stationed in the Pacific, you know. Always said the humidity out there would rust a man's bones if he wasn't careful. He brought back this beautiful carved wooden box from the Philippines. You have to see the whole pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you think others at your workplace share your belief that management is building a paper trail against you?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know if anyone else sees it. I mean, nobody's come out and said it to me. But honestly, most of them are so young they wouldn't know a targeted audit if it bit them. They're all fresh out of those boot camps, you know? Six weeks of coding and suddenly they're system engineers. \n\nWhen I went to college, we actually had to learn the theory behind it all. I remember my first computer science professor, Dr. Henderson. He had this massive, unruly beard and always wore these tweed jackets with elbow patches. Classic academic type. He used to say that if you didn't understand how the hardware worked, you had no business writing software. He actually made us build a simple logic board from scratch before we ever touched a keyboard. I burned my fingers on the soldering iron three times that semester. Still have a tiny scar on my left thumb from it. \n\nI actually tried to teach my son how to solder when he was in middle school, but he just wasn't interested. He was all about his skateboard. He broke his arm trying to do some trick off a set of stairs at the park. We spent six hours in the emergency room that night. The vending machine was completely out of everything except those weird cheese crackers that taste like cardboard. \n\nAnyway, what was I... oh, right, the other people at work. Yeah, I don't think they see the pattern. They just blindly follow the ticket system and don't question why I'm getting all the high-risk assignments. They're too busy talking about whatever new app they downloaded.\n\nDoctor: Do you think management is specifically targeting you for any particular reason?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know why me, specifically. I mean, I'm good at my job, I've been there forever. Maybe I know too much about the legacy systems. They hate the legacy systems. They want everything in the cloud now. \n\nI remember when \"the cloud\" just meant it was going to rain. My grandmother used to say she could smell the rain coming, and she was always right. She grew up on a farm in Nebraska, out where the sky is just... huge. You can see weather rolling in from miles away. We drove out there once when I was a kid, took the station wagon all the way across the country. That car had those wood panels on the sides, you know the ones? And no air conditioning. My brother and I stuck to the vinyl seats the whole way through Kansas. We played the license plate game for hours, trying to find all fifty states. I think we only ever missed Hawaii and Alaska. We did see a plate from Guam once, though, in a diner parking lot in Omaha. The diner had the best cherry pie I've ever eaten. Real cherries, not that canned syrup stuff. \n\nBut, uh... wait. Targeting me. Right. I don't know the exact reason. I just know they're doing it. You don't build a paper trail that thick unless you're trying to push someone out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think management's actions are intended to harm you personally?\n\nPatient: Well, harm me personally? I don't know if they want to physically hurt me, but they definitely want to ruin my career. I'm here because for over a year now, I've been trying to get someone to take this seriously. The new ticket rotation, those random security audits... they're using them to build this massive paper trail against me. \n\nAnd they keep pulling me into these meetings, showing me all these system logs and telling me, \"Oh, it's just standard policy, everyone goes through this.\" But I know it's not true. \n\nSpeaking of policies, my homeowners association just passed this ridiculous new rule about the height of the grass. They sent out this newsletter with a literal ruler printed on the side of it, like we're supposed to go out there and measure our lawns every Tuesday. The guy who runs the HOA, Bob, he's got way too much time on his hands since he retired from the post office. He used to be a mail carrier in my old neighborhood, actually. Walked the same route for thirty years. He always had these little dog treats in his pocket. My old golden retriever, Buster, used to wait by the window every afternoon just for Bob. Buster was such a good dog. We got him from a shelter when he was already three years old. He had this funny little limp on his back left leg from an old injury, but it didn't slow him down one bit. He loved swimming in the lake behind our house. \n\nBut, yeah, the management at work. They say it's just policy, but I know what they're doing. They're trying to push me out, and I just want someone to finally listen to me about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think management's actions against you are part of a larger conspiracy?\n\nPatient: A larger conspiracy? I mean... I haven't seen anything to suggest that it goes beyond my department. I don't think the government or the Illuminati or whoever cares about a legacy systems guy at a mid-level tech firm. It's just my management. \n\nAlthough, speaking of conspiracies, my brother-in-law, Dave, he's completely obsessed with all that UFO stuff. He spends hours on these internet forums reading about declassified military videos. Last Thanksgiving, he brought this huge binder full of printed out articles and tried to show them to everyone while we were eating the turkey. My sister was so embarrassed, she kept trying to change the subject to her new garden. She planted these heirloom tomatoes last spring, the big purple ones? Cherokee Purples, I think they're called. They make the best sandwiches. Just some thick white bread, a little mayonnaise, some salt and pepper. You don't need anything else. I used to grow tomatoes when I had my first house, but the squirrels kept getting to them before they were ripe. Little thieves. I tried putting up netting, I tried that pepper spray stuff you put on the leaves, nothing worked. They just chewed right through the nets. \n\nBut no, I don't think there's any grand conspiracy. It's just the people above me at work trying to force me out so they can hire someone cheaper who doesn't know any better.\n\nDoctor: If someone could prove to you that the ticket rotation and security audits are indeed just standard company policy, would you be able to accept that explanation?\n\nPatient: Prove it to me? I mean... how could they? I've been doing this for a year. A whole year of trying to get someone, anyone, to take this seriously. They already show me the system logs, they already sit there in those little glass conference rooms and tell me, \"Oh, it's just policy, everyone has the same ticket rotation, everyone gets these security audits.\" But I know what I'm looking at. They're just using it to build that paper trail against me. \n\nYou know, those glass conference rooms are terrible for acoustics. You can hear everything from the hallway. We had this one manager, Greg, who used to chew ice during meetings. Just crunch, crunch, crunch, right into the microphone on conference calls. It drove everyone crazy. Greg ended up leaving to start a microbrewery up in Vermont or somewhere like that. He was always talking about hops. I don't really get the appeal of those super bitter IPAs, honestly. I prefer a nice, simple lager if I'm going to have a beer. My dad used to drink Pabst Blue Ribbon. He'd sit on the porch after work with a can of PBR and listen to the baseball game on this tiny little transistor radio he had. The reception was awful, just static half the time, but he knew all the players' stats by heart. He could tell you who was batting just by the sound of the announcer's voice through the fuzz. \n\nBut... no, to answer your question, I wouldn't accept it. Because they already try to prove it to me with their logs and their policies, and I know they're lying. The paper trail is real, and it's aimed right at me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I tried putting up netting, I tried that pepper spray stuff you put on the leaves, nothing worked. They just chewed right through the nets. \n\nBut no, I don't think there's any grand conspiracy. It's just the people above me at work trying to force me out so they can hire someone cheaper who doesn't know any better.\n\nDoctor: If someone could prove to you that the ticket rotation and security audits are indeed just standard company policy, would you be able to accept that explanation?\n\nPatient: Prove it to me? I mean... how could they? I've been doing this for a year. A whole year of trying to get someone, anyone, to take this seriously. They already show me the system logs, they already sit there in those little glass conference rooms and tell me, \"Oh, it's just policy, everyone has the same ticket rotation, everyone gets these security audits.\" But I know what I'm looking at. They're just using it to build that paper trail against me. \n\nYou know, those glass conference rooms are terrible for acoustics. You can hear everything from the hallway. We had this one manager, Greg, who used to chew ice during meetings. Just crunch, crunch, crunch, right into the microphone on conference calls. It drove everyone crazy. Greg ended up leaving to start a microbrewery up in Vermont or somewhere like that. He was always talking about hops. I don't really get the appeal of those super bitter IPAs, honestly. I prefer a nice, simple lager if I'm going to have a beer. My dad used to drink Pabst Blue Ribbon. He'd sit on the porch after work with a can of PBR and listen to the baseball game on this tiny little transistor radio he had. The reception was awful, just static half the time, but he knew all the players' stats by heart. He could tell you who was batting just by the sound of the announcer's voice through the fuzz. \n\nBut... no, to answer your question, I wouldn't accept it. Because they already try to prove it to me with their logs and their policies, and I know they're lying. The paper trail is real, and it's aimed right at me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think there's something wrong with your own thinking or perception that might be contributing to your beliefs about work?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 60 / computer_support_specialist / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Wrong with my thinking? No, no, my mind is perfectly clear. I mean, my perception is fine. I see exactly what they're doing. If anything, I'm the only one who's actually paying attention to what's going on around there. 

You know, paying attention... that reminds me of this time I had to fix the motherboard on my neighbor's computer. This was back in the late nineties, when everyone was getting those big beige tower PCs. He had spilled coffee right through the top vent. Just a total disaster. But I took the whole thing apart, cleaned every single contact with rubbing alcohol and these tiny little cotton swabs. Took me all weekend. I missed the Super Bowl that year because of it. I think it was the Broncos and the Falcons? Yeah, Elway's last game. My buddy Steve had this huge party with a seven-layer dip and everything, and I was stuck in my basement smelling like isopropyl alcohol. Steve was always throwing parties. He had this amazing built-in grill on his patio that he built himself out of river rock. He was a stonemason by trade, did a lot of the retaining walls in our subdivision. Really nice guy, but he moved to Arizona a few years ago because the winters here were getting too hard on his joints. 

But anyway, no, my thinking is fine. I'm not crazy. I just know what they're doing to me at work, and I'm not going to let them get away with it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, my primary doctor suggested I come in. I’m here because... well, for over a year now, I’ve been trying to get someone, anyone really, to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work. I've been in computer support for a long time, you know, over thirty years. Back in the nineties, we didn't have these automated ticketing systems. We actually walked over to people's desks to fix things. I remember I had this one user, Susan, who used to bring in homemade zucchini bread every Tuesday. She used walnuts, which I thought was a nice touch. You just don't see that kind of office culture anymore. Now everyone just stares at their screens and drinks that terrible pod coffee in the breakroom. I actually brought in my own French press last week, but someone complained to HR about the grounds in the sink. People are so petty. But anyway, management keeps pulling me in, showing me these system logs and saying the new ticket assignments are just company policy, but I know what they're really doing. I mean, if you're going to complain about coffee grounds, at least have the courage to leave a note, right?\n\nDoctor: Do you really believe that your workplace is building a paper trail against you?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, absolutely I believe it. Like I was just saying, it's been going on for over a year now. They pull me into these little conference rooms and point at all these system logs, giving me this rehearsed speech about how the new ticket rotation and the security audits are just standard company policy. The noise they made was incredible, just this loud mechanical screeching, and you had to carefully tear off those perforated edges with the little holes in them. I actually kept a box of that old continuous-feed paper in my garage for the longest time. We even took her to this farm out in the county where she got to ride a pony named Macaroni... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the logs and the audits. Yeah, management can call it policy all they want, but I'm not stupid. I know exactly what they're building against me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself jumping from one topic to another like you did just now, from talking about work to talking about printers and your daughter's birthday?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, it's just how conversations work, isn't it? One thing reminds you of another. Like, you mention printers, and it just takes me right back. Honestly, I think people today are too focused on getting straight to the point. They don't appreciate a good story. \n\nMy grandfather, now he was a storyteller. He used to sit on the porch for hours, whittling these little wooden figures—mostly bears and eagles—and he'd tell us these grand tales about his time in the Navy. He was stationed in the Pacific, you know. Always said the humidity out there would rust a man's bones if he wasn't careful. He brought back this beautiful carved wooden box from the Philippines. My sister has it now. She always was his favorite, which I never really understood because she never even liked the wooden figures he made. She was more into collecting those porcelain dolls. You know the ones with the creepy glass eyes that seem to follow you around the room? My grandmother had a whole cabinet full of them, and I swear, I couldn't sleep when we visited because I thought they were watching me. \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't think I jump around. I'm just trying to give you the full picture of who I am. You can't understand a person if you only look at one little piece of them, right? Just like you can't understand what management is doing if you only look at one ticket. You have to see the whole pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you think others at your workplace share your belief that management is building a paper trail against you?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know if anyone else sees it. I mean, nobody's come out and said it to me. But honestly, most of them are so young they wouldn't know a targeted audit if it bit them. They're all fresh out of those boot camps, you know? Six weeks of coding and suddenly they're system engineers. \n\nWhen I went to college, we actually had to learn the theory behind it all. I remember my first computer science professor, Dr. Henderson. He had this massive, unruly beard and always wore these tweed jackets with elbow patches. Classic academic type. He used to say that if you didn't understand how the hardware worked, you had no business writing software. He actually made us build a simple logic board from scratch before we ever touched a keyboard. I burned my fingers on the soldering iron three times that semester. Still have a tiny scar on my left thumb from it. \n\nI actually tried to teach my son how to solder when he was in middle school, but he just wasn't interested. He was all about his skateboard. He broke his arm trying to do some trick off a set of stairs at the park. We spent six hours in the emergency room that night. The vending machine was completely out of everything except those weird cheese crackers that taste like cardboard. \n\nAnyway, what was I... oh, right, the other people at work. Yeah, I don't think they see the pattern. They just blindly follow the ticket system and don't question why I'm getting all the high-risk assignments. They're too busy talking about whatever new app they downloaded.\n\nDoctor: Do you think management is specifically targeting you for any particular reason?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know why me, specifically. I mean, I'm good at my job, I've been there forever. Maybe I know too much about the legacy systems. They hate the legacy systems. They want everything in the cloud now. \n\nI remember when \"the cloud\" just meant it was going to rain. My grandmother used to say she could smell the rain coming, and she was always right. She grew up on a farm in Nebraska, out where the sky is just... huge. You can see weather rolling in from miles away. We drove out there once when I was a kid, took the station wagon all the way across the country. That car had those wood panels on the sides, you know the ones? And no air conditioning. My brother and I stuck to the vinyl seats the whole way through Kansas. We played the license plate game for hours, trying to find all fifty states. I think we only ever missed Hawaii and Alaska. We did see a plate from Guam once, though, in a diner parking lot in Omaha. The diner had the best cherry pie I've ever eaten. Real cherries, not that canned syrup stuff. \n\nBut, uh... wait. Targeting me. Right. I don't know the exact reason. I just know they're doing it. You don't build a paper trail that thick unless you're trying to push someone out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think management's actions are intended to harm you personally?\n\nPatient: Well, harm me personally? I don't know if they want to physically hurt me, but they definitely want to ruin my career. I'm here because for over a year now, I've been trying to get someone to take this seriously. The new ticket rotation, those random security audits... they're using them to build this massive paper trail against me. \n\nAnd they keep pulling me into these meetings, showing me all these system logs and telling me, \"Oh, it's just standard policy, everyone goes through this.\" But I know it's not true. \n\nSpeaking of policies, my homeowners association just passed this ridiculous new rule about the height of the grass. They sent out this newsletter with a literal ruler printed on the side of it, like we're supposed to go out there and measure our lawns every Tuesday. The guy who runs the HOA, Bob, he's got way too much time on his hands since he retired from the post office. He used to be a mail carrier in my old neighborhood, actually. Walked the same route for thirty years. He always had these little dog treats in his pocket. My old golden retriever, Buster, used to wait by the window every afternoon just for Bob. Buster was such a good dog. We got him from a shelter when he was already three years old. He had this funny little limp on his back left leg from an old injury, but it didn't slow him down one bit. He loved swimming in the lake behind our house. \n\nBut, yeah, the management at work. They say it's just policy, but I know what they're doing. They're trying to push me out, and I just want someone to finally listen to me about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think management's actions against you are part of a larger conspiracy?\n\nPatient: A larger conspiracy? Change my mind? No, because like I was saying, they already try to show me things. Management keeps showing me the system logs and saying it's just standard policy. I'm here because for over a year now, I've been trying to get someone, anyone, to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work. 

Speaking of paper trails, you wouldn't believe the literal mountains of paper we used to go through back when we had those old dot-matrix printers. You know, the ones with the perforated edges you had to tear off the sides? The noise those things made was deafening. I used to have to change the ink ribbons on those all the time. You'd get that black ink all over your fingers, and it would just not come off. My ex-wife hated it. She'd make me scrub my hands with that gritty orange pumice soap that mechanics use before I could even sit down at the dinner table. We had this gorgeous solid oak dining table, bought it at an estate sale from a couple who were moving down to Florida. 

I could never do Florida, honestly. Way too humid. And the bugs down there are the size of small birds. My cousin went to Orlando for a tech conference once and said a palmetto bug flew right into the open window of his rental car while he was on the highway. He was driving a Chevy Malibu, I think. Nice cars, good gas mileage, but the sight lines in the rear-view mirror are just terrible. I prefer my old Honda. It’s got over two hundred thousand miles on it and it still runs like a top. 

But no, there's nothing they could show me. They can keep saying it's just policy and waving their logs in my face all they want, but I know exactly what they're doing. I just need someone to finally take it seriously.

They already show me the system logs, they already sit there in those little glass conference rooms and tell me, \"Oh, it's just policy, everyone has the same ticket rotation, everyone gets these security audits.\" But I know what I'm looking at. They already show me the system logs, they already sit there in those little glass conference rooms and tell me, \"Oh, it's just policy, everyone has the same ticket rotation, everyone gets these security audits.\" But I know what I'm looking at. They're just using it to build that paper trail against me. \n\nYou know, those glass conference rooms are terrible for acoustics. You can hear everything from the hallway. We had this one manager, Greg, who used to chew ice during meetings. Just crunch, crunch, crunch, right into the microphone on conference calls. It drove everyone crazy. Greg ended up leaving to start a microbrewery up in Vermont or somewhere like that. He was always talking about hops. I don't really get the appeal of those super bitter IPAs, honestly. I prefer a nice, simple lager if I'm going to have a beer. My dad used to drink Pabst Blue Ribbon. He'd sit on the porch after work with a can of PBR and listen to the baseball game on this tiny little transistor radio he had. The reception was awful, just static half the time, but he knew all the players' stats by heart. He could tell you who was batting just by the sound of the announcer's voice through the fuzz. \n\nBut... no, to answer your question, I wouldn't accept it. Because they already try to prove it to me with their logs and their policies, and I know they're lying. The paper trail is real, and it's aimed right at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's something wrong with your own thinking or perception that might be contributing to your beliefs about work?\n\nPatient: Wrong with my thinking? No, no, my mind is perfectly clear. I mean, my perception is fine. I see exactly what they're doing. If anything, I'm the only one who's actually paying attention to what's going on around there. \n\nYou know, paying attention... that reminds me of this time I had to fix the motherboard on my neighbor's computer. This was back in the late nineties, when everyone was getting those big beige tower PCs. He had spilled coffee right through the top vent. Just a total disaster. But I took the whole thing apart, cleaned every single contact with rubbing alcohol and these tiny little cotton swabs. Took me all weekend. I missed the Super Bowl that year because of it. I think it was the Broncos and the Falcons? Yeah, Elway's last game. My buddy Steve had this huge party with a seven-layer dip and everything, and I was stuck in my basement smelling like isopropyl alcohol. Steve was always throwing parties. He had this amazing built-in grill on his patio that he built himself out of river rock. He was a stonemason by trade, did a lot of the retaining walls in our subdivision. Really nice guy, but he moved to Arizona a few years ago because the winters here were getting too hard on his joints. \n\nBut anyway, no, my thinking is fine. I'm not crazy. I just know what they're doing to me at work, and I'm not going to let them get away with it.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that could happen or anything someone could show you that would make you change your mind about management building a paper trail against you?\n\nPatient: Change my mind? I'm here because for over a year now, I've been trying to get someone, anyone, to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work. \n\nSpeaking of paper trails, you wouldn't believe the literal mountains of paper we used to go through back when we had those old dot-matrix printers. You know, the ones with the perforated edges you had to tear off the sides? The noise those things made was deafening. I used to have to change the ink ribbons on those all the time. You'd get that black ink all over your fingers, and it would just not come off. My ex-wife hated it. She'd make me scrub my hands with that gritty orange pumice soap that mechanics use before I could even sit down at the dinner table. We had this gorgeous solid oak dining table, bought it at an estate sale from a couple who were moving down to Florida. \n\nI could never do Florida, honestly. Way too humid. And the bugs down there are the size of small birds. My cousin went to Orlando for a tech conference once and said a palmetto bug flew right into the open window of his rental car while he was on the highway. He was driving a Chevy Malibu, I think. Nice cars, good gas mileage, but the sight lines in the rear-view mirror are just terrible. I prefer my old Honda. It’s got over two hundred thousand miles on it and it still runs like a top. \n\nBut no, there's nothing they could show me. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
My closest colleagues? You mean the guys I sit next to every day? No, I... I don't think that would change anything. Because, like I've been saying, I've been at this for over a year. A whole year of trying to get someone to take this seriously. And management keeps showing me the logs and saying it's just policy, that the new ticket rotation and security audits are just standard procedure. So if my colleagues said they didn't see it, they'd just be buying into the same lie, or maybe they're just not looking close enough. 

You know, looking close enough... that's the problem with people these days. Nobody pays attention to the details. Like when you're trying to read the serial numbers on the back of those old Cisco routers. The print was so small, you practically needed a magnifying glass. I used to keep one of those jeweler's loupes in my desk drawer just for that. Got it at a flea market out by the old drive-in theater. That drive-in was great, they used to do double features on Friday nights. I saw *Jurassic Park* there when it first came out. The sound system was terrible, you had to hook this heavy metal speaker onto your car window, and it always sounded like they were talking through a tin can. But the popcorn was amazing. They used real butter, not that yellow oil they pump out of a jug nowadays. My stomach can't handle that fake stuff anymore. Gives me terrible heartburn. I have to sleep propped up on three pillows if I eat anything greasy after 7 PM. 

But yeah, my colleagues... they're good guys, mostly, but they're just keeping their heads down. They don't want to see what's actually happening with the ticket rotations and the audits. They don't want to believe that management is building a paper trail against me, even though it's right there in front of them.

My sister has it now. She always was his favorite, which I never really understood because she never even liked the wooden figures he made. She was more into collecting those porcelain dolls. You know the ones with the creepy glass eyes that seem to follow you around the room? My grandmother had a whole cabinet full of them, and I swear, I couldn't sleep when we visited because I thought they were watching me. \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't think I jump around. I'm just trying to give you the full picture of who I am. You can't understand a person if you only look at one little piece of them, right? Just like you can't understand what management is doing if you only look at one ticket. You have to see the whole pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you think others at your workplace share your belief that management is building a paper trail against you?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know if anyone else sees it. I mean, nobody's come out and said it to me. But honestly, most of them are so young they wouldn't know a targeted audit if it bit them. They're all fresh out of those boot camps, you know? Six weeks of coding and suddenly they're system engineers. \n\nWhen I went to college, we actually had to learn the theory behind it all. I remember my first computer science professor, Dr. Henderson. He had this massive, unruly beard and always wore these tweed jackets with elbow patches. Classic academic type. He used to say that if you didn't understand how the hardware worked, you had no business writing software. He actually made us build a simple logic board from scratch before we ever touched a keyboard. I burned my fingers on the soldering iron three times that semester. Still have a tiny scar on my left thumb from it. \n\nI actually tried to teach my son how to solder when he was in middle school, but he just wasn't interested. He was all about his skateboard. He broke his arm trying to do some trick off a set of stairs at the park. We spent six hours in the emergency room that night. The vending machine was completely out of everything except those weird cheese crackers that taste like cardboard. \n\nAnyway, what was I... oh, right, the other people at work. Yeah, I don't think they see the pattern. They just blindly follow the ticket system and don't question why I'm getting all the high-risk assignments. They're too busy talking about whatever new app they downloaded.\n\nDoctor: Do you think management is specifically targeting you for any particular reason?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know why me, specifically. I mean, I'm good at my job, I've been there forever. Maybe I know too much about the legacy systems. They hate the legacy systems. They want everything in the cloud now. \n\nI remember when \"the cloud\" just meant it was going to rain. My grandmother used to say she could smell the rain coming, and she was always right. She grew up on a farm in Nebraska, out where the sky is just... huge. You can see weather rolling in from miles away. We drove out there once when I was a kid, took the station wagon all the way across the country. That car had those wood panels on the sides, you know the ones? And no air conditioning. My brother and I stuck to the vinyl seats the whole way through Kansas. We played the license plate game for hours, trying to find all fifty states. I think we only ever missed Hawaii and Alaska. We did see a plate from Guam once, though, in a diner parking lot in Omaha. The diner had the best cherry pie I've ever eaten. Real cherries, not that canned syrup stuff. \n\nBut, uh... wait. Targeting me. Right. I don't know the exact reason. I just know they're doing it. You don't build a paper trail that thick unless you're trying to push someone out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think management's actions are intended to harm you personally?\n\nPatient: Well, harm me personally? I don't know if they want to physically hurt me, but they definitely want to ruin my career. I'm here because for over a year now, I've been trying to get someone to take this seriously. The new ticket rotation, those random security audits... they're using them to build this massive paper trail against me. \n\nAnd they keep pulling me into these meetings, showing me all these system logs and telling me, \"Oh, it's just standard policy, everyone goes through this.\" But I know it's not true. \n\nSpeaking of policies, my homeowners association just passed this ridiculous new rule about the height of the grass. They sent out this newsletter with a literal ruler printed on the side of it, like we're supposed to go out there and measure our lawns every Tuesday. The guy who runs the HOA, Bob, he's got way too much time on his hands since he retired from the post office. He used to be a mail carrier in my old neighborhood, actually. Walked the same route for thirty years. He always had these little dog treats in his pocket. My old golden retriever, Buster, used to wait by the window every afternoon just for Bob. Buster was such a good dog. We got him from a shelter when he was already three years old. He had this funny little limp on his back left leg from an old injury, but it didn't slow him down one bit. He loved swimming in the lake behind our house. \n\nBut, yeah, the management at work. They say it's just policy, but I know what they're doing. They're trying to push me out, and I just want someone to finally listen to me about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think management's actions against you are part of a larger conspiracy?\n\nPatient: A larger conspiracy? I mean... I haven't seen anything to suggest that it goes beyond my department. I don't think the government or the Illuminati or whoever cares about a legacy systems guy at a mid-level tech firm. It's just my management. \n\nAlthough, speaking of conspiracies, my brother-in-law, Dave, he's completely obsessed with all that UFO stuff. He spends hours on these internet forums reading about declassified military videos. Last Thanksgiving, he brought this huge binder full of printed out articles and tried to show them to everyone while we were eating the turkey. My sister was so embarrassed, she kept trying to change the subject to her new garden. She planted these heirloom tomatoes last spring, the big purple ones? Cherokee Purples, I think they're called. They make the best sandwiches. Just some thick white bread, a little mayonnaise, some salt and pepper. You don't need anything else. I used to grow tomatoes when I had my first house, but the squirrels kept getting to them before they were ripe. Little thieves. I tried putting up netting, I tried that pepper spray stuff you put on the leaves, nothing worked. They just chewed right through the nets. \n\nBut no, I don't think there's any grand conspiracy. It's just the people above me at work trying to force me out so they can hire someone cheaper who doesn't know any better.\n\nDoctor: If someone could prove to you that the ticket rotation and security audits are indeed just standard company policy, would you be able to accept that explanation?\n\nPatient: Prove it to me? I mean... how could they? I've been doing this for a year. A whole year of trying to get someone, anyone, to take this seriously. They already show me the system logs, they already sit there in those little glass conference rooms and tell me, \"Oh, it's just policy, everyone has the same ticket rotation, everyone gets these security audits.\" But I know what I'm looking at. They're just using it to build that paper trail against me. \n\nYou know, those glass conference rooms are terrible for acoustics. You can hear everything from the hallway. We had this one manager, Greg, who used to chew ice during meetings. Just crunch, crunch, crunch, right into the microphone on conference calls. It drove everyone crazy. Greg ended up leaving to start a microbrewery up in Vermont or somewhere like that. He was always talking about hops. I don't really get the appeal of those super bitter IPAs, honestly. I prefer a nice, simple lager if I'm going to have a beer. My dad used to drink Pabst Blue Ribbon. He'd sit on the porch after work with a can of PBR and listen to the baseball game on this tiny little transistor radio he had. The reception was awful, just static half the time, but he knew all the players' stats by heart. He could tell you who was batting just by the sound of the announcer's voice through the fuzz. \n\nBut... no, to answer your question, I wouldn't accept it. Because they already try to prove it to me with their logs and their policies, and I know they're lying. The paper trail is real, and it's aimed right at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's something wrong with your own thinking or perception that might be contributing to your beliefs about work?\n\nPatient: Wrong with my thinking? No, no, my mind is perfectly clear. I mean, my perception is fine. I see exactly what they're doing. If anything, I'm the only one who's actually paying attention to what's going on around there. \n\nYou know, paying attention... that reminds me of this time I had to fix the motherboard on my neighbor's computer. This was back in the late nineties, when everyone was getting those big beige tower PCs. He had spilled coffee right through the top vent. Just a total disaster. But I took the whole thing apart, cleaned every single contact with rubbing alcohol and these tiny little cotton swabs. Took me all weekend. I missed the Super Bowl that year because of it. I think it was the Broncos and the Falcons? Yeah, Elway's last game. My buddy Steve had this huge party with a seven-layer dip and everything, and I was stuck in my basement smelling like isopropyl alcohol. Steve was always throwing parties. He had this amazing built-in grill on his patio that he built himself out of river rock. He was a stonemason by trade, did a lot of the retaining walls in our subdivision. Really nice guy, but he moved to Arizona a few years ago because the winters here were getting too hard on his joints. \n\nBut anyway, no, my thinking is fine. I'm not crazy. I just know what they're doing to me at work, and I'm not going to let them get away with it.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that could happen or anything someone could show you that would make you change your mind about management building a paper trail against you?\n\nPatient: Change my mind? No, because like I was saying, they already try to show me things. Management keeps showing me the system logs and saying it's just standard policy. I'm here because for over a year now, I've been trying to get someone, anyone, to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work. \n\nSpeaking of paper trails, you wouldn't believe the literal mountains of paper we used to go through back when we had those old dot-matrix printers. You know, the ones with the perforated edges you had to tear off the sides? The noise those things made was deafening. I used to have to change the ink ribbons on those all the time. You'd get that black ink all over your fingers, and it would just not come off. My ex-wife hated it. She'd make me scrub my hands with that gritty orange pumice soap that mechanics use before I could even sit down at the dinner table. We had this gorgeous solid oak dining table, bought it at an estate sale from a couple who were moving down to Florida. \n\nI could never do Florida, honestly. Way too humid. And the bugs down there are the size of small birds. My cousin went to Orlando for a tech conference once and said a palmetto bug flew right into the open window of his rental car while he was on the highway. He was driving a Chevy Malibu, I think. Nice cars, good gas mileage, but the sight lines in the rear-view mirror are just terrible. I prefer my old Honda. It’s got over two hundred thousand miles on it and it still runs like a top. \n\nBut no, there's nothing they could show me. They can keep saying it's just policy and waving their logs in my face all they want, but I know exactly what they're doing. I just need someone to finally take it seriously.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about the workplace conspiracy would change if everyone at work, including your closest colleagues, told you they see no evidence of it?\n\nPatient: My closest colleagues? You mean the guys I sit next to every day? No, I... I don't think that would change anything. Because, like I've been saying, I've been at this for over a year. A whole year of trying to get someone to take this seriously. And management keeps showing me the logs and saying it's just policy, that the new ticket rotation and security audits are just standard procedure. So if my colleagues said they didn't see it, they'd just be buying into the same lie, or maybe they're just not looking close enough. \n\nYou know, looking close enough... that's the problem with people these days. Nobody pays attention to the details. Like when you're trying to read the serial numbers on the back of those old Cisco routers. The print was so small, you practically needed a magnifying glass. I used to keep one of those jeweler's loupes in my desk drawer just for that. Got it at a flea market out by the old drive-in theater. That drive-in was great, they used to do double features on Friday nights. I saw *Jurassic Park* there when it first came out. The sound system was terrible, you had to hook this heavy metal speaker onto your car window, and it always sounded like they were talking through a tin can. But the popcorn was amazing. They used real butter, not that yellow oil they pump out of a jug nowadays. My stomach can't handle that fake stuff anymore. Gives me terrible heartburn. I have to sleep propped up on three pillows if I eat anything greasy after 7 PM. \n\nBut yeah, my colleagues... they're good guys, mostly, but they're just keeping their heads down. They don't want to see what's actually happening with the ticket rotations and the audits. They don't want to believe that management is building a paper trail against me, even though it's right there in front of them.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Question my belief? No. I mean, why would I question it when it's happening right in front of me? I've been saying this for over a year now. Over a year of trying to get someone, anyone, to take it seriously that they're using the new ticket rotation and the security audits to build this paper trail against me. Management just keeps waving those logs in my face, telling me it's standard policy. 

Standard policy... you know, that reminds me of the policies we had back when I first started in IT. We had this strict dress code. You had to wear a tie every single day. Can you imagine? A tie, while you're crawling under desks trying to fish out a loose Ethernet cable. I ruined so many good ties that way. Catching them on the edges of those old metal desks. Those desks were heavy, too. Solid steel. You needed three guys just to move one across the room. My buddy Dave threw his back out trying to push one by himself. He was out for six weeks, had to go to physical therapy and everything. He used to complain about the co-pay for those sessions. Said it was highway robbery. Dave ended up buying this special ergonomic chair for his home office, one of those ones where you kneel on it? Looks like a torture device, if you ask me. I tried sitting in it once and nearly pitched forward right onto my face. 

But no, I'm not going to question it. They can say it's policy all they want, but I know what the audits and the ticket rotations are really for. I just need someone to finally see it.
