My buddy Dave threw his back out trying to push one by himself. He was out for six weeks, had to go to physical therapy and everything. He used to complain about the co-pay for those sessions. Said it was highway robbery. Dave ended up buying this special ergonomic chair for his home office, one of those ones where you kneel on it? Looks like a torture device, if you ask me. I tried sitting in it once and nearly pitched forward right onto my face. \n\nBut no, I'm not going to question it. They can say it's policy all they want, but I know what the audits and the ticket rotations are really for. I just need someone to finally see it.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that could ever make you doubt your belief that management is building a paper trail against you?\n\nPatient: Doubt it? No, absolutely not. I mean, how many times do I have to say it? It's been over a year. A whole year of me practically begging someone to just look at what's going on. Management keeps pulling up these system logs, pointing at the screen, telling me, \"Oh, it's just standard policy.\" But I know exactly what that new ticket rotation and those security audits are actually doing. They're building a paper trail, plain and simple. \n\nPaper trails... you know, my grandfather used to keep a paper trail of everything. Literally everything. He had these stacks of yellow legal pads in his study. He'd write down every penny he spent at the grocery store, the hardware store, you name it. He used those old fountain pens, the ones you had to fill from a little glass bottle of ink. He had this beautiful Parker 51, dark blue with a silver cap. I always wanted that pen, but when he passed away, my brother ended up taking it. Typical. My brother always swooped in and took the best stuff. He took the grandfather clock, too. That thing chimed every hour, drove my grandmother absolutely crazy. She used to stuff cotton balls in her ears just to get through the night. She bought those cotton balls in bulk from this old pharmacy downtown that smelled like mothballs and peppermint. \n\nBut anyway, no. There is no doubt in my mind. They can call it policy all they want, but I know they're coming after me with those audits. I just need someone to finally take it seriously.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your interpretation of events at work might be incorrect?\n\nPatient: Incorrect? No. I mean, we've been over this, Doc. For over a year, I've been trying to get someone to take this seriously. The new ticket rotation, the security audits... it's all right there. They're building a paper trail against me, and they just keep showing me the same logs, over and over, telling me it's just policy. \n\nIt's funny they use the word policy. My first car insurance policy was with this local guy, Mr. Henderson. He had an office above the old bakery on Main Street. You'd go in there to pay your premium, and the whole place smelled like fresh sourdough and cinnamon rolls. I bought my first car from my uncle, a '78 Ford Pinto. Orange. Bright, ugly orange. The heater didn't work, so in the winter, I had to scrape the frost off the *inside* of the windshield while I was driving. I used an old cassette tape case to do it. Remember cassettes? I had this huge binder full of them in the backseat. Mostly classic rock, some blues. I had this one Muddy Waters tape that I played so much the ribbon finally snapped. I tried splicing it back together with scotch tape, but it always skipped right at the best part of \"Mannish Boy.\" \n\nBut yeah, no. I'm not incorrect about this. I know what I'm seeing with the audits and the tickets. I just need someone to actually look at it and take it seriously, instead of just hiding behind their \"policy.\"\n\nDoctor: Would you still believe management is building a paper trail against you even if an independent investigator found no evidence of it?\n\nPatient: An independent investigator? Honestly, Doc, yes, I would still believe it. Because I know what I'm seeing. Like I keep telling you, it's been over a year of me trying to get *anyone* to take this seriously. An investigator is just going to look at the same logs management keeps showing me, the ones they swear are just their \"standard policy.\" But I know the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. \n\nInvestigators, though... you know, my neighbor hired a private investigator once. Back in '98, I think. He thought his wife was stealing his vintage coin collection. Turns out she was just taking them to the bank for safe keeping. But this investigator, he parked on our street every day and drove this beat-up old Chevy Nova. You don't see Novas on the road anymore. My cousin Jimmy had a Nova in high school. We took it down to the lake one summer and the alternator died right in the parking lot. We had to hike three miles to a gas station in the blistering heat. Jimmy bought a lukewarm soda from the vending machine, one of those old glass bottles of RC Cola. You had to use the metal bottle opener bolted to the front of the machine. I always liked that. It felt... I don't know, substantial. Not like the flimsy plastic bottles they sell today. You drop one of those and it just bounces. My recycling bin is full of them, mostly from the sparkling water my daughter leaves at my house. \n\nBut anyway, no, an investigator wouldn't change a thing. Management would just show them the exact same logs and feed them the same line about it being policy, but it doesn't change the fact that they are building a case against me with those audits.\n\nDoctor: Would anything, no matter how convincing, ever make you reconsider your belief that management is out to get you?\n\nPatient: Reconsider it? Honestly, Doc, I don't see how. I mean, I'm here because for over a year now, I've been trying to get *someone* to take this seriously. The new ticket rotation, those security audits... they are being used to build a paper trail against me at work. And it doesn't matter what anyone says, because management just keeps showing me the same logs and saying it's just policy. \n\nPolicy... it's a funny word, isn't it? My dog, Buster, he had a strict policy about the mail carrier. Every day at 11:30, he'd sit by the front window, just waiting. He was a golden retriever mix, had this one floppy ear that never quite stood up right. He loved tennis balls, but he wouldn't fetch them. He'd just chew on them until they were completely bald. I used to buy those big bags of them from the sporting goods store over by the mall. That mall is mostly empty now, which is a shame. They used to have this great pretzel place right in the center court. You could smell the butter and salt all the way from the parking lot. I'd always get the cinnamon sugar one, though. It came in this little white paper sleeve that would get translucent from the grease. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, nothing is going to make me reconsider. I know what they're doing with those audits, and I just need someone to finally see it for what it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your belief about management building a paper trail against you is not based in reality?\n\nPatient: Not based in reality? Doc, we've been over and over this. Look, I'm here, sitting in this chair, because for over a year now I've been trying to get *someone*—anyone—to take this seriously. The new ticket rotation, the security audits... it's all right there. They are being used to build a paper trail against me at work. And they just keep pointing to the same logs, telling me it's just policy. \n\nReality... you know, reality TV is just terrible these days. My wife used to watch that show with the people stranded on the island. Survivor, I think it was called. The first season was okay, they actually had to catch fish and eat bugs. Richard Hatch, remember him? Walked around naked half the time. I never understood why they didn't just build a better shelter. I took a woodworking class at the community college back in '85. Built a beautiful oak coffee table. Took me six months to get the dovetail joints just right. My daughter has it in her apartment now. She lives over in the Heights, near that old Italian place, Luigi's. Best chicken parm in the city. They use real mozzarella, not that rubbery stuff you get at the supermarket. I bought a block of cheese the other day that didn't even melt. Put it in the microwave for two minutes and it just sat there, sweating. \n\nBut anyway, no, my belief is completely based in reality. I know what they're doing with the ticket rotation, and I know what those audits mean, no matter how many times they claim it's just their policy.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything, no matter how small, that could potentially make you question or doubt your belief that management is building a paper trail against you?\n\nPatient: Doubt it? Doc, I feel like a broken record here, but no. There isn't anything. I'm sitting in this office because for over a year now, I’ve been trying to get someone—anyone—to take it seriously that the new ticket rotation and these security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work. And I don't care that management keeps showing me logs and saying it’s just policy, because I know exactly what I'm looking at.\n\nA paper trail... you know, speaking of paper, back when I first started in IT, we didn't have these quiet little laser printers. We had those massive dot-matrix ones. The noise they made, it was like a machine gun going off in the office. And they used that continuous form paper with the perforated strips on the sides. Tractor-feed, we called it. I used to tear off those strips and take them home for my kids, and they'd use them as confetti. My son used to make a huge mess in the living room with them. We had this thick shag carpet back then, a sort of burnt orange color. Vacuuming that thing was an absolute nightmare. The vacuum cleaner we had was this heavy metal Hoover, weighed a ton. It had a little headlight on the front, which I always thought was hilarious. Why do you need a headlight to vacuum a living room? It's not like you're driving down the highway at midnight. I drove through the night once, all the way to Chicago for a tech conference. Stopped at this diner in Indiana at 3 AM and had the best slice of cherry pie I've ever eaten in my life. The waitress called everyone \"hon\" and kept topping off my coffee without me even asking. \n\nBut anyway... no. There's no small thing that's going to make me question it. I know they're building that paper trail, and management can hide behind their logs and their \"policy\" all they want. I just need someone to finally believe me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Doc, are you testing me? Because I feel like you're just asking me the exact same question but using slightly different words. No, it's not possible that it's not true. Like I keep saying, I am here because for over a year now, I've been desperately trying to get someone to take this seriously. The new ticket rotation, those security audits... they are being used to build a paper trail against me at work. And it doesn't matter that management keeps showing me logs and saying it's just policy, because I know what they're really doing.

True... you know, speaking of what's true, they used to say "the camera never lies." But with all this AI stuff now, you can't trust a single picture you see on the internet. Back in the day, if you wanted to alter a photo, you had to actually spend hours in a darkroom. I took a photography elective when I was getting my bachelor's. We had to roll our own film onto these little plastic spools in pitch black. If you messed up, the whole roll was exposed and ruined. It always smelled like vinegar in there because of the stop bath chemical. I actually dropped my favorite camera, a Canon AE-1, right into a puddle outside the student union building. Cracked the lens right down the middle. Cost me fifty bucks to fix, which was a fortune back then. I was making minimum wage working at a record store off campus. People say vinyl is making a big comeback now, but I don't think it's the same. Kids today buy them at big box stores, they don't know the joy of flipping through dusty crates for hours just to find that one specific album you've been hunting for. We used to have this regular customer, a guy named Earl, who came in every Tuesday looking for obscure jazz imports... 

But anyway, what was I saying? Right, my job. No, I'm not wrong about this. I know what I'm seeing with those tickets and audits, and no amount of them hiding behind their logs and calling it "policy" is going to make it any less true.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, my primary doctor suggested I come in. I’m here because... well, for over a year now, I’ve been trying to get someone, anyone really, to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work. I've been in computer support for a long time, you know, over thirty years. Back in the nineties, we didn't have these automated ticketing systems. We actually walked over to people's desks to fix things. I remember I had this one user, Susan, who used to bring in homemade zucchini bread every Tuesday. She used walnuts, which I thought was a nice touch. You just don't see that kind of office culture anymore. Now everyone just stares at their screens and drinks that terrible pod coffee in the breakroom. I know what I'm seeing with those tickets and audits, and no amount of them hiding behind their logs and calling it \"policy\" is going to make it any less true."
